### GreenMind: A Next-Generation Vietnamese Large Language Model for Structured and Logical Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16832v1)
- **Authors**: Luu Quy Tung, Hoang Quoc Viet, Vo Trong Thu
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) is a robust approach for tackling LLM tasks that require intermediate reasoning steps prior to generating a final answer. In this paper, we present GreenMind-Medium-14B-R1, the Vietnamese reasoning model inspired by the finetuning strategy based on Group Relative Policy Optimization. We also leverage a high-quality Vietnamese synthesized reasoning dataset and design two reward functions to tackle the main limitations of this technique: (i) language mixing, where we explicitly detect the presence of biased language characters during the process of sampling tokens, and (ii) we leverage Sentence Transformer-based models to ensure that the generated reasoning content maintains factual correctness and does not distort the final output. Experimental results on the Vietnamese dataset from the VLSP 2023 Challenge demonstrate that our model outperforms prior works and enhances linguistic consistency in its responses. Furthermore, we extend our evaluation to SeaExam-a multilingual multiple-choice dataset, showing the effectiveness of our reasoning method compared to few-shot prompting techniques.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the GreenMind paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GreenMind-Medium-14B-R1, a Vietnamese Large Language Model (LLM) specifically designed for structured and logical reasoning.  It builds upon the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) technique and incorporates a finetuning strategy based on Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). A key aspect is the creation of a high-quality, Vietnamese-synthesized reasoning dataset and the design of two reward functions to address limitations of existing techniques: language mixing (biased towards English/Chinese) and factual correctness of reasoning. The model is evaluated on Vietnamese datasets (VLSP 2023 Challenge) and multilingual datasets (SeaExam), demonstrating improved performance and linguistic consistency compared to previous approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has moderate novelty. Using GRPO for LLM finetuning isn't entirely new, but applying it specifically to a Vietnamese LLM and addressing language-specific biases is a valuable contribution. The creation of a high-quality Vietnamese reasoning dataset is a significant asset. The reward functions targeting language bias and factual correctness in the context of Vietnamese language modeling are also important.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses an important gap: the lack of robust, natively-focused LLMs for Vietnamese and other Southeast Asian languages.  While LLMs are becoming increasingly powerful, their performance often lags for languages with less training data. GreenMind's focus on Vietnamese language and logical reasoning could have a substantial impact on applications in that region. Demonstrating improved performance on the VLSP challenge is good, but more comparisons against a broader range of Vietnamese-focused models would be even stronger. The results on SeaExam are also positive in that the proposed model outperforms previous methods.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a real need for better Vietnamese LLMs.
    *   The created dataset is a valuable resource for the community.
    *   The language and reasoning reward functions are a good way to mitigate common issues.
    *   Demonstrated improvements in performance on relevant datasets.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The description of the GRPO implementation could be more detailed.
    *   The comparisons to existing models in the experimental section (especially other Vietnamese-focused models) could be more comprehensive.
    *   While the SeaExam results are promising, more detailed analysis of the types of questions where GreenMind excels would be beneficial.
    * The writing could be made more succinct and clear in some sections.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more effective LLMs for resource-limited languages. The dataset and techniques developed could be adopted by other researchers in the field. The focus on factual correctness and linguistic purity are also important contributions that could encourage better practices in the broader LLM community.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 7. The paper has several important strengths, including addressing a real gap in Vietnamese language modeling and creating a new dataset. The use of reward functions to improve language consistency and factual correctness shows a good understanding of the problems in current LLMs. However, the lack of thorough comparison to all relevant existing models, especially when it comes to Vietnamese, and a lack of detail in the methods section, detract from the impact. It is worth noting that the paper clearly highlights the need for community focus and development by providing the source data and code for others to contribute.

### LRASGen: LLM-based RESTful API Specification Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16833v1)
- **Authors**: Sida Deng, Rubing Huang, Man Zhang, Chenhui Cui, Dave Towey, Rongcun Wang
- **Abstract**: REpresentation State Transfer (REST) is an architectural style for designing web applications that enable scalable, stateless communication between clients and servers via common HTTP techniques. Web APIs that employ the REST style are known as RESTful (or REST) APIs. When using or testing a RESTful API, developers may need to employ its specification, which is often defined by open-source standards such as the OpenAPI Specification (OAS). However, it can be very time-consuming and error-prone to write and update these specifications, which may negatively impact the use of RESTful APIs, especially when the software requirements change. Many tools and methods have been proposed to solve this problem, such as Respector and Swagger Core. OAS generation can be regarded as a common text-generation task that creates a formal description of API endpoints derived from the source code. A potential solution for this may involve using Large Language Models (LLMs), which have strong capabilities in both code understanding and text generation. Motivated by this, we propose a novel approach for generating the OASs of RESTful APIs using LLMs: LLM-based RESTful API-Specification Generation (LRASGen). To the best of our knowledge, this is the first use of LLMs and API source code to generate OASs for RESTful APIs. Compared with existing tools and methods, LRASGen can generate the OASs, even when the implementation is incomplete (with partial code, and/or missing annotations/comments, etc.). To evaluate the LRASGen performance, we conducted a series of empirical studies on 20 real-world RESTful APIs. The results show that two LLMs (GPT-4o mini and DeepSeek V3) can both support LARSGen to generate accurate specifications, and LRASGen-generated specifications cover an average of 48.85% more missed entities than the developer-provided specifications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LRASGen: LLM-based RESTful API Specification Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LRASGen, a novel approach for automatically generating OpenAPI specifications (OAS) for RESTful APIs using Large Language Models (LLMs).  LRASGen analyzes API source code, identifies endpoint methods, parameters, constraints, and responses, and then leverages LLMs (specifically GPT-40 mini and DeepSeek V3) to generate OAS documents. The authors compared LRASGen against developer-provided specifications and three state-of-the-art OAS generation tools (Respector, Swagger Core, Springdoc). The results demonstrate that LRASGen can generate accurate and complete OASs, often exceeding the completeness of developer-written specifications and outperforming existing tools, especially in handling different programming languages and identifying parameter constraints.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of applying LLMs to RESTful API specification generation is moderately novel. While LLMs have been used for code understanding and generation tasks, this paper specifically targets OAS generation.  The novelty partly lies in the systematic approach designed for code extraction, prompting, and results processing to address the specific challenges of API specification generation.  However, similar ideas exploring code documentation and generation exist in the broader LLM literature.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is substantial.  Generating accurate and up-to-date API specifications is a persistent problem in software development. If LRASGen's performance holds up in practice and can be further improved, it could significantly reduce the burden on developers and improve API usability, testability, and security. The tool's ability to handle different languages and frameworks adds value. The discovery that LLMs can often identify elements that humans missed from their API specifications is also an important finding.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors performed a rigorous evaluation, using a set of 20 real-world RESTful APIs written in various languages and frameworks. The comparative analysis against state-of-the-art tools provides a strong empirical foundation.
    *   **Detailed Methodology:**  The paper provides a detailed explanation of LRASGen's workflow, including the prompt engineering strategies, which are crucial for the reproducibility and adaptability of the approach.
    *   **Addressing Limitations of Existing Tools:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing tools like Respector, Swagger Core and Springdoc, and demonstrates that LRASGen overcomes some of these limitations.
    *   **Publicly Available GT\*** The public availability of the improved ground truth provides the community the opportunity to build upon this work and evaluate their own tools/techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Dependency:**  The approach is inherently dependent on the capabilities, cost, and availability of specific LLMs (GPT-40 mini and DeepSeek V3 in this case). Changes in LLM technology could impact LRASGen's performance and cost-effectiveness. Furthermore, the LLM choice is only justified based on indicator scores and cost. A deeper study justifying the choice of these LLMs based on their ability to accomplish the key tasks of code understanding and text generation would further strengthen the paper.
    *   **Potential Bias and Generalizability:** Even though the study is performed over 20 real-world RESTful APIs, there is no discussion on whether or not the sample covers a diverse set of domains. If the API samples come from a limited set of domains, the generalizability of the approach across other domains would be an issue.
    *   **Limited Error Analysis:** The paper presents aggregate results but lacks a detailed error analysis. Knowing the types of errors LRASGen makes would be valuable for guiding future improvements.
    *   **Lack of Quantitative Comparison**: While the paper mentions existing works, there is a limited quantitative comparison against existing techniques like static analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can motivate further research on using LLMs for API-related tasks, especially in automated documentation, testing, and security analysis.  It provides a concrete example of how LLMs can be integrated into existing software development workflows. The open-source implementation and GT* will further encourage adoption and research.

*   **Overall:**

The paper presents a well-executed study demonstrating the potential of LLMs for API specification generation.  While it has some limitations regarding the dependency on LLMs, a deep justification of the choice of LLMs and a detailed quantitative analysis, its strengths in methodology and empirical evaluation justify a positive assessment.

### Physically Consistent Humanoid Loco-Manipulation using Latent Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16843v1)
- **Authors**: Ilyass Taouil, Haizhou Zhao, Angela Dai, Majid Khadiv
- **Abstract**: This paper uses the capabilities of latent diffusion models (LDMs) to generate realistic RGB human-object interaction scenes to guide humanoid loco-manipulation planning. To do so, we extract from the generated images both the contact locations and robot configurations that are then used inside a whole-body trajectory optimization (TO) formulation to generate physically consistent trajectories for humanoids. We validate our full pipeline in simulation for different long-horizon loco-manipulation scenarios and perform an extensive analysis of the proposed contact and robot configuration extraction pipeline. Our results show that using the information extracted from LDMs, we can generate physically consistent trajectories that require long-horizon reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Physically Consistent Humanoid Loco-Manipulation using Latent Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel pipeline for humanoid loco-manipulation planning that leverages the capabilities of Latent Diffusion Models (LDMs).  The approach uses LDMs to generate realistic 2D RGB images of human-object interaction scenes based on high-level text prompts.  From these generated images, the pipeline extracts 3D contact locations and robot configurations.  This information is then used within a whole-body trajectory optimization (TO) formulation to generate physically consistent and geometrically feasible trajectories for a humanoid robot to perform complex loco-manipulation tasks. The paper validates the approach in simulation, demonstrating its ability to solve long-horizon tasks that would be difficult for traditional methods. The paper emphasizes the ability to generate physically consistent trajectories and long-horizon reasoning capabilities through LDM information.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of the paper lies in integrating LDMs for generating human-like demonstrations that serve as a guide for a humanoid robot performing similar tasks. While using generative models to guide robotic planning isn't entirely new, the specific combination of LDMs for scene generation, coupled with the contact and robot configuration extraction, and its subsequent use in a whole-body TO framework represents a significant contribution. It alleviates the need for task-specific heuristics and large 3D annotated datasets, which are common limitations in existing approaches. The combination of techniques distinguishes this work.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its potential to address the challenges of planning long-horizon, complex loco-manipulation tasks for humanoid robots. Humanoids are notoriously difficult to control due to their high dimensionality and unstable dynamics. The proposed method offers a promising way to leverage the understanding of human behavior, implicitly encoded in LDMs, to guide the robot's motion planning. The demonstration of long-horizon scenarios is important.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Integration of multiple techniques:**  The paper effectively integrates LDMs, contact transfer, retargeting, and trajectory optimization into a coherent and functional pipeline.
    *   **Long-horizon planning:**  The method demonstrates the ability to solve tasks that require long-horizon reasoning, a significant advantage over local planning methods like TO alone.
    *   **Simulation validation:**  The approach is validated through simulation with detailed analysis with different baselines.
    *   **Demonstration of handling complex scenarios:** The two chosen scenarios (basket retrieval and trolley pushing) are demonstrably complex and require coordinated locomotion and manipulation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on simulation:**  The paper only presents results in simulation. While the simulation environment is realistic, transferring the approach to a real-world humanoid robot will likely introduce significant challenges related to perception noise, model inaccuracies, and control execution. This remains a key area for future validation.
    *   **Sensitivity to LDM quality:** The performance of the pipeline is directly dependent on the quality of the images generated by the LDMs.  The contact transfer is based on these generated images and if the generated images do not have accurate object representations the pipeline may suffer.  This is a common limitation of any approach relying on generative models.
    *   **Contact transfer inaccuracies:**  While the paper refines the contact extraction, errors in object detection, depth estimation (especially for generated images), and pose estimation can still lead to inaccurate contact locations. These inaccuracies can negatively impact the TO result.
    *   **Limited exploration of LDM prompting:** The paper only briefly describes the LDM prompting strategy.  A more thorough investigation into the effect of different prompts on the final trajectory could be valuable.
    *   **Heuristics for contact location:** The contact location correction heuristic that projects the hand and feet onto the closest surface seems to be applied in a rather arbitrary manner. Also, using LDMs for a more explicit handling of contact locations could be interesting.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the approach can be successfully transferred to real-world humanoid robots, it has the potential to significantly advance the field of humanoid loco-manipulation. It offers a means of bridging the gap between high-level task specifications and low-level robot control, enabling humanoid robots to perform more complex and useful tasks in unstructured environments. Additionally, it opens avenues for researching the use of other generative models (e.g., video generation) to guide robot planning.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite the existing limitations, the paper presents a novel and promising approach to a challenging problem. The effective integration of LDMs with traditional robotics techniques, coupled with the demonstration of long-horizon planning in simulation, justifies a score above average. The novelty in leveraging LDMs specifically for generating guidance cues and the significant impact it *could* have if successfully translated to real-world implementations merit the score.

### Hyperspectral Vision Transformers for Greenhouse Gas Estimations from Space
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16851v1)
- **Authors**: Ruben Gonzalez Avilés, Linus Scheibenreif, Nassim Ait Ali Braham, Benedikt Blumenstiel, Thomas Brunschwiler, Ranjini Guruprasad, Damian Borth, Conrad Albrecht, Paolo Fraccaro, Devyani Lambhate, Johannes Jakubik
- **Abstract**: Hyperspectral imaging provides detailed spectral information and holds significant potential for monitoring of greenhouse gases (GHGs). However, its application is constrained by limited spatial coverage and infrequent revisit times. In contrast, multispectral imaging offers broader spatial and temporal coverage but often lacks the spectral detail that can enhance GHG detection. To address these challenges, this study proposes a spectral transformer model that synthesizes hyperspectral data from multispectral inputs. The model is pre-trained via a band-wise masked autoencoder and subsequently fine-tuned on spatio-temporally aligned multispectral-hyperspectral image pairs. The resulting synthetic hyperspectral data retain the spatial and temporal benefits of multispectral imagery and improve GHG prediction accuracy relative to using multispectral data alone. This approach effectively bridges the trade-off between spectral resolution and coverage, highlighting its potential to advance atmospheric monitoring by combining the strengths of hyperspectral and multispectral systems with self-supervised deep learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a spectral transformer model to synthesize hyperspectral data from multispectral inputs, aiming to address the limitations of each imaging modality. Hyperspectral imaging provides detailed spectral information useful for monitoring greenhouse gases (GHGs), but has limited spatial coverage and infrequent revisit times. Multispectral imaging offers broader coverage but lacks the spectral detail needed for accurate GHG detection.  The proposed model is pre-trained using a band-wise masked autoencoder and then fine-tuned on spatio-temporally aligned multispectral-hyperspectral image pairs.  The synthetic hyperspectral data is shown to retain the spatial and temporal benefits of multispectral imagery while improving GHG prediction accuracy compared to using multispectral data alone. The approach aims to bridge the gap between spectral resolution and coverage for advanced atmospheric monitoring.  The method is evaluated using EnMAP and Sentinel-2 datasets, along with GHG measurements from Sentinel-5P and OCO-3.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of synthesizing hyperspectral data from multispectral data isn't entirely new; however, the paper's use of a spectral transformer architecture with a masked autoencoder pre-training strategy for this specific purpose is a novel contribution. The paper's strength lies in how they adapt transformer architectures, commonly used in natural language processing, to the spectral domain, which leverages self-attention mechanisms for reconstructing spectral information.  The band-wise masking strategy during pre-training is a crucial element, helping the model to learn inter-band dependencies.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is considerable. Accurately monitoring GHGs is crucial for understanding and mitigating climate change. Bridging the gap between high-resolution spectral data and broad spatial/temporal coverage is a key challenge. The proposed method has the potential to improve GHG monitoring by leveraging the strengths of both hyperspectral and multispectral systems. The paper provides empirical evidence that the synthetic hyperspectral data improves GHG estimation relative to using multispectral data alone, which directly addresses this trade-off.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of both hyperspectral and multispectral imaging and the need for a solution that combines their advantages.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:** The spectral transformer architecture, the masked autoencoder pre-training, and the fine-tuning strategy are well-defined and justified.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper uses multiple datasets (EnMAP, Sentinel-2, Sentinel-5P, OCO-3) and several evaluation metrics to assess the performance of the model.  The use of both real and synthetic Sentinel-2 data adds robustness to the analysis.  The use of both "easy" and "hard" splits for training and testing demonstrates concern for avoiding data leakage.
    *   **Ablation study**: The evaluation includes an ablation study on the effect of using the pre-trained weights as opposed to random initialization.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **CO2 Results are Inconclusive**: The results for CO2 detection are less convincing than those for methane or NO2, with reconstructed data outperforming original hyperspectral data. The authors acknowledge this and attribute it to the small dataset size, which should be addressed in future work.
    *   **Limited Downstream Task Diversity**:  While GHG estimation is an important application, exploring other downstream tasks (e.g., vegetation classification, mineral mapping) could further showcase the versatility of the reconstructed hyperspectral data.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to Other Super-resolution Methods**: The paper mentions the Spectral-Learning-Based transformer network as related work, and it should be included in the experimental results section.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can significantly influence the field of remote sensing, particularly in atmospheric monitoring and environmental assessment. The proposed approach provides a practical way to enhance the utility of existing multispectral datasets, and the model architecture itself can serve as a basis for future research on spectral super-resolution and data fusion. The code will be released to the public.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to an important problem in remote sensing. The model architecture is innovative, the experimental evaluation is comprehensive, and the results are promising. While some limitations exist (particularly regarding CO2 results and limited downstream task diversity), the overall contribution is significant and has the potential to advance the field.

### Monte Carlo Planning with Large Language Model for Text-Based Game Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16855v1)
- **Authors**: Zijing Shi, Meng Fang, Ling Chen
- **Abstract**: Text-based games provide valuable environments for language-based autonomous agents. However, planning-then-learning paradigms, such as those combining Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) and reinforcement learning (RL), are notably time-consuming due to extensive iterations. Additionally, these algorithms perform uncertainty-driven exploration but lack language understanding and reasoning abilities. In this paper, we introduce the Monte Carlo planning with Dynamic Memory-guided Large language model (MC-DML) algorithm. MC-DML leverages the language understanding and reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) alongside the exploratory advantages of tree search algorithms. Specifically, we enhance LLMs with in-trial and cross-trial memory mechanisms, enabling them to learn from past experiences and dynamically adjust action evaluations during planning. We conduct experiments on a series of text-based games from the Jericho benchmark. Our results demonstrate that the MC-DML algorithm significantly enhances performance across various games at the initial planning phase, outperforming strong contemporary methods that require multiple iterations. This demonstrates the effectiveness of our algorithm, paving the way for more efficient language-grounded planning in complex environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Monte Carlo Planning with Large Language Model for Text-Based Game Agents":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces Monte Carlo Planning with Dynamic Memory-guided Large language model (MC-DML), a novel algorithm that integrates Large Language Models (LLMs) into Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) for text-based game agents. MC-DML enhances LLMs with in-trial and cross-trial memory mechanisms, enabling the agent to learn from past experiences and dynamically adjust action evaluations during planning. The experiments, conducted on the Jericho benchmark, demonstrate that MC-DML enhances performance across various games, particularly in the initial planning phase, outperforming existing methods that rely on extensive iterations. The paper also provides ablation studies to highlight the role of the memory mechanism in LLM policy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The integration of LLMs into MCTS is not entirely new. Prior work has explored LLMs as a prior policy.  However, the *dynamic* memory mechanism, both in-trial and cross-trial, to guide LLM-based action evaluation during MCTS is a key differentiator. The idea of using failure trajectories to adapt the LLM's decision-making process is promising.  Compared to existing MCTS+RL methods, this avoids a lengthy "planning-then-learning" phase by directly using the LLM as a foundation. However, the prompt engineering aspect, while crucial, is not deeply explored from a purely prompt innovation perspective.
*   **Significance:** Text-based games are complex environments for language-based AI. MC-DML shows a performance boost at the initial planning phase. More efficient language grounding can pave the way for more advanced AI agents capable of solving complex tasks. The focus on addressing the limitations of traditional MCTS (lack of language understanding and reasoning) is relevant and timely. The success in Jericho benchmark environments adds credence to the approach.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The concept of dynamic memory (in-trial and cross-trial) is well-motivated and seems to contribute significantly to the performance improvement.
    *   The approach addresses a practical issue: the warm-up period needed for traditional MCTS+RL.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate a clear improvement over several strong baselines.
    *   Ablation studies help clarify the contribution of the dynamic memory components.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The implementation details of the prompt engineering for both action evaluation and reflection could be more detailed.  It's not clear how robust the performance is to different prompt formulations.
    *   The analysis, while highlighting the bottleneck solving in *Zork1*, is limited. More qualitative analysis or case studies on other games would strengthen the claims.
    *   The "limitations" section mentions the LLM's "Needle In a Haystack" ability but doesn't explore this aspect experimentally. Future work should quantify the impact of longer-term in-trial memories.
    *   The approach still relies on GPT-3.5, which is a significant dependency. Exploration of smaller or more efficient LLMs in this context would increase practical applicability.
*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence research on language-grounded AI, particularly in the context of interactive tasks. The idea of dynamically adapting LLM priors in tree search is general and could be applied to other domains. The dynamic memory mechanism could inspire novel approaches to improving LLM reasoning in interactive environments. The paper is clearly written and presents a strong empirical evaluation.

**Justification for Score:**

While the core idea of using an LLM within MCTS isn't entirely new, the introduction of dynamic memory (both in-trial and, more importantly, cross-trial) and its integration into action evaluation represents a substantial improvement. The empirical results convincingly show the effectiveness of MC-DML. The weaknesses, such as limited prompt exploration and reliance on a specific LLM, don't invalidate the core contribution but rather point to future research directions. Overall, the paper presents a well-executed and impactful approach to language-grounded planning.

### Emo Pillars: Knowledge Distillation to Support Fine-Grained Context-Aware and Context-Less Emotion Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16856v1)
- **Authors**: Alexander Shvets
- **Abstract**: Most datasets for sentiment analysis lack context in which an opinion was expressed, often crucial for emotion understanding, and are mainly limited by a few emotion categories. Foundation large language models (LLMs) like GPT-4 suffer from over-predicting emotions and are too resource-intensive. We design an LLM-based data synthesis pipeline and leverage a large model, Mistral-7b, for the generation of training examples for more accessible, lightweight BERT-type encoder models. We focus on enlarging the semantic diversity of examples and propose grounding the generation into a corpus of narratives to produce non-repetitive story-character-centered utterances with unique contexts over 28 emotion classes. By running 700K inferences in 450 GPU hours, we contribute with the dataset of 100K contextual and also 300K context-less examples to cover both scenarios. We use it for fine-tuning pre-trained encoders, which results in several Emo Pillars models. We show that Emo Pillars models are highly adaptive to new domains when tuned to specific tasks such as GoEmotions, ISEAR, IEMOCAP, and EmoContext, reaching the SOTA performance on the first three. We also validate our dataset, conducting statistical analysis and human evaluation, and confirm the success of our measures in utterance diversification (although less for the neutral class) and context personalization, while pointing out the need for improved handling of out-of-taxonomy labels within the pipeline.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Emo Pillars Π: Knowledge Distillation to Support Fine-Grained Context-Aware and Context-Less Emotion Classification":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of existing sentiment analysis datasets, which often lack contextual information and are limited to a small number of emotion categories. To overcome these challenges, the authors present a method for generating a large, diverse emotion classification dataset using LLMs. They propose a pipeline that grounds the generation process in story narratives, incorporates various characters' perspectives, and encourages the creation of multiple utterances simultaneously. This approach aims to enhance semantic diversity and reduce repetitive outputs. The generated data is used to fine-tune BERT-type encoder models, resulting in "Emo Pillars Π" models that achieve state-of-the-art performance on several emotion classification tasks, demonstrating their adaptability to new domains. They conduct statistical analysis, human evaluation, and transfer learning experiments to validate and enhance their datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the creation of a diverse and scalable emotion classification dataset through LLM-based data synthesis, grounding the generation in story narratives and persona.
    *   **Significance:** The dataset addresses a significant need for context-aware emotion recognition resources. The models trained on the generated data achieve competitive results on benchmark datasets, indicating the effectiveness of the data synthesis approach.
    *   **Technical Soundness:** The authors detail the generation pipeline, training procedures, and evaluation metrics. The experiments are comprehensive, including intra-dataset evaluation, transfer learning to existing datasets, and human evaluation.
    *   **Thoroughness:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the generated dataset, including emotion distribution, topic coverage, and semantic diversity.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Bias:** Although mitigated to some extent, the synthetic data is still dependent on the priors and biases of the Mistral LLM model. This can create limitations and restrict the generalizability of the trained models, especially considering they relied on one model, as mentioned by the authors.
    *   **Neutral Class:** The paper acknowledges challenges in diversifying the neutral class, which could impact the performance of models in accurately classifying non-emotional utterances.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** While the use of macro-averaged metrics is appreciated, using only the F1-score can hide important properties of the model, specifically with precision and recall.
    *   **Ethics and bias:** The paper touches on this subject, however, the dependence on the pre-training of the model on english-centered data sets can limit the model’s generalization, and the ethics concerning this issue might require more attention.
    *   **Overgeneralization:** The claim that the fine-tuned models surpass prior SOTA results is not justified due to the method of comparing and the limitations of prior models and therefore can be considered misleading.

*   **Impact:**

    *   The generated dataset has the potential to significantly impact the field of emotion recognition by providing a valuable resource for training context-aware models.
    *   The data synthesis pipeline could be adapted and extended to other domains, providing a generalizable approach for creating training data.

**Justification for the score:**

The paper presents a well-executed approach for addressing the data scarcity problem in fine-grained emotion classification. The novelty of the data synthesis pipeline, grounding the generation process in the context of story narratives is a major contribution. The generated dataset is evaluated thoroughly and models trained on the data demonstrate strong performance on several benchmarks. However, the limitations regarding potential biases and data distribution are not negligible.

### Planning with Diffusion Models for Target-Oriented Dialogue Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16858v1)
- **Authors**: Hanwen Du, Bo Peng, Xia Ning
- **Abstract**: Target-Oriented Dialogue (TOD) remains a significant challenge in the LLM era, where strategic dialogue planning is crucial for directing conversations toward specific targets. However, existing dialogue planning methods generate dialogue plans in a step-by-step sequential manner, and may suffer from compounding errors and myopic actions. To address these limitations, we introduce a novel dialogue planning framework, DiffTOD, which leverages diffusion models to enable non-sequential dialogue planning. DiffTOD formulates dialogue planning as a trajectory generation problem with conditional guidance, and leverages a diffusion language model to estimate the likelihood of the dialogue trajectory. To optimize the dialogue action strategies, DiffTOD introduces three tailored guidance mechanisms for different target types, offering flexible guidance towards diverse TOD targets at test time. Extensive experiments across three diverse TOD settings show that DiffTOD can effectively perform non-myopic lookahead exploration and optimize action strategies over a long horizon through non-sequential dialogue planning, and demonstrates strong flexibility across complex and diverse dialogue scenarios. Our code and data are accessible through https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DiffTOD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Planning with Diffusion Models for Target-Oriented Dialogue Systems":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DiffTOD, a novel dialogue planning framework for target-oriented dialogue (TOD) systems.  Instead of sequential, step-by-step dialogue plan generation, DiffTOD leverages diffusion models to enable non-sequential dialogue planning.  It formulates dialogue planning as a trajectory generation problem, using a diffusion language model to estimate the likelihood of dialogue trajectories. The key innovation lies in the conditional guidance mechanisms tailored for different target types, providing flexible control during inference to steer the conversation towards complex and diverse goals.  The paper demonstrates through experiments across multiple TOD settings that DiffTOD outperforms baselines in target achievement, showing better flexibility and lookahead reasoning capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of using diffusion models for non-sequential dialogue planning is significantly novel.  While diffusion models have been used for text generation, applying them to the *planning* aspect of TOD, and particularly in a non-Markovian way to avoid compounding errors, is a substantial contribution. The three guidance mechanisms further enhance the practicality and adaptability of the approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing Limitations:** The paper directly tackles a major limitation of LLM-powered TOD agents: the lack of proactive planning.
    *   **Non-Sequential Planning:** The move away from the traditional Markovian assumption in dialogue planning allows for better lookahead and global consistency.
    *   **Flexible Guidance:** The target-specific guidance mechanisms are well-designed, allowing for flexible application to diverse scenarios and preventing the need for re-training for different target types.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The extensive experiments across diverse datasets (CraigslistBargain, TopDial, PersonaChat) provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of DiffTOD. The empirical results justify the theoretical innovation.
    *   **Tackling sparse rewards:** In tasks such as negotiation, reward feedback is given at the end. Existing works that generate dialogs in sequence find it difficult to generate good dialogs. By enabling non-sequential dialogue planning conditioned on the target state, this approach is able to tackle this problem.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models are computationally expensive compared to standard autoregressive decoding, and the paper acknowledges this limitation.  While the experiments show practical feasibility on A100 GPUs, the inference cost could be a barrier to wider adoption.
    *   **Reliance on Simulated Users:**  The evaluation, particularly for TopDial, relies heavily on simulated user interactions.  While this is common in the field, it's a simplification of real-world user behavior.
    *   **Evaluation of Text Quality:** In the experiments, the LLM-generated dialog quality is evaluated using BLEU, word-level F1 and BERTScore. These measurements may not accurately reflect the coherence and effectiveness of human language.
    *   **Dynamic adjustment of the dialogue plans:** The framework leverages diffusion models to simulate transitions. There is a possibility that the simulated environment may not align with the real world, and there is no mechanism for adjusting the plans.

**Significance:** The paper presents a significant advance in dialogue planning for TOD systems. It offers a promising alternative to existing methods by leveraging diffusion models. This could have a substantial impact on how LLM-powered dialogue agents are designed and trained, especially in scenarios requiring strategic, long-term planning. If the computational cost can be further reduced, DiffTOD has the potential to become a standard approach for TOD.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** DiffTOD makes a significant contribution to dialogue planning by introducing a novel framework based on diffusion models. The approach addresses limitations of existing methods, such as sequential planning and myopic actions, and demonstrates strong empirical results across diverse TOD settings. However, the computational cost and reliance on simulated users are noteworthy weaknesses that limit its immediate impact. With further research on reducing inference cost and validating the approach with real user interactions, DiffTOD has the potential to become a highly influential approach in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Exploring How LLMs Capture and Represent Domain-Specific Knowledge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16871v1)
- **Authors**: Mirian Hipolito Garcia, Camille Couturier, Daniel Madrigal Diaz, Ankur Mallick, Anastasios Kyrillidis, Robert Sim, Victor Ruhle, Saravan Rajmohan
- **Abstract**: We study whether Large Language Models (LLMs) inherently capture domain-specific nuances in natural language. Our experiments probe the domain sensitivity of LLMs by examining their ability to distinguish queries from different domains using hidden states generated during the prefill phase. We reveal latent domain-related trajectories that indicate the model's internal recognition of query domains. We also study the robustness of these domain representations to variations in prompt styles and sources. Our approach leverages these representations for model selection, mapping the LLM that best matches the domain trace of the input query (i.e., the model with the highest performance on similar traces). Our findings show that LLMs can differentiate queries for related domains, and that the fine-tuned model is not always the most accurate. Unlike previous work, our interpretations apply to both closed and open-ended generative tasks
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper explores how Large Language Models (LLMs) capture and represent domain-specific knowledge. The authors investigate the "domain sensitivity" of LLMs by analyzing hidden states generated during the prefill phase. They reveal latent domain-related trajectories in these hidden states that indicate the model's internal recognition of query domains. The paper also examines the robustness of these representations to prompt style variations and different sources of queries. A model selection approach based on these representations is presented, where the LLM that best matches the domain trace of an input query is chosen (essentially, selecting the model with the best performance on similar traces). The findings suggest LLMs can differentiate queries from related domains, and, surprisingly, a domain-fine-tuned model is not always the most accurate.  The authors emphasize that their interpretation extends to both closed and open-ended generative tasks, unlike some prior work.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The core idea of leveraging LLM hidden states as domain representations is potentially novel, specifically extending it to open-ended tasks. Prior work often focuses on probing specific factual knowledge or closed-ended scenarios. The finding that domain-fine-tuned models aren't always superior when using hidden states for model selection is also intriguing and somewhat counter-intuitive.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its attempt to provide a more transparent understanding of how LLMs adapt their reasoning across different domains. This is important for high-stakes applications where reliability and explainability are crucial. The proposed method for model selection, if robust, could have practical implications for optimizing LLM performance in various real-world scenarios. It also offers a new interpretation to the LLM representation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Research Question:** The paper directly addresses the important question of how LLMs adapt to different domains.
*   **Thorough Experimentation:** The authors explore multiple LLM architectures (Gemma, Phi, Mistral, Llama) and a pre-trained encoder model (DeBERTa) to investigate different representation capabilities.
*   **Interesting Findings:** The paper reveals the existence of latent domain-related trajectories and the robustness of these traces even after fine-tuning.  The improved model selection approach is promising.
*   **Comparative Analysis:** Comparing their approach with traditional semantic and token-based methods helps to highlight the potential advantages of hidden state representations.
*   **Well-defined terminology:** Paper has well-defined definitions, and terminology, making it easier to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The paper primarily focuses on smaller LLMs (up to 7B parameters). The applicability of the findings to larger, more complex models needs further investigation.
*   **Domain Relationship Concerns:** The observation that the mathematical and biomedical domains are closely related raises questions about the distinctiveness of the domain representations. This could limit the method's effectiveness in scenarios requiring finer-grained domain differentiation.
*   **Interpretability:**  While the paper demonstrates the existence of domain-related trajectories, the *meaning* of these trajectories remains somewhat opaque.  The paper falls short of providing a deep understanding of what specific features in the hidden states are responsible for domain recognition. The paper lacks explainability of the representation.
*   **Performance Gain Origin:** It is unclear why the approach works. There are some hints and clues, but not a firm conclusion.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach to understanding LLM behavior and a potentially useful model selection technique. The findings are interesting and the experimentation is reasonably thorough. However, the limitations regarding model size, domain distinctiveness, and interpretability need to be addressed. The paper also lacks in explaining why this happens, or why this works. While significant, its limited scope and lack of clarity on the reasons behind its findings prevent it from achieving a higher score.
Therefore, the study has interesting observations, but it doesn't fully reach a conclusion.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Context-Enhanced Vulnerability Detection Based on Large Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16877v1)
- **Authors**: Yixin Yang, Bowen Xu, Xiang Gao, Hailong Sun
- **Abstract**: Vulnerability detection is a critical aspect of software security. Accurate detection is essential to prevent potential security breaches and protect software systems from malicious attacks. Recently, vulnerability detection methods leveraging deep learning and large language models (LLMs) have garnered increasing attention. However, existing approaches often focus on analyzing individual files or functions, which limits their ability to gather sufficient contextual information. Analyzing entire repositories to gather context introduces significant noise and computational overhead. To address these challenges, we propose a context-enhanced vulnerability detection approach that combines program analysis with LLMs. Specifically, we use program analysis to extract contextual information at various levels of abstraction, thereby filtering out irrelevant noise. The abstracted context along with source code are provided to LLM for vulnerability detection. We investigate how different levels of contextual granularity improve LLM-based vulnerability detection performance. Our goal is to strike a balance between providing sufficient detail to accurately capture vulnerabilities and minimizing unnecessary complexity that could hinder model performance. Based on an extensive study using GPT-4, DeepSeek, and CodeLLaMA with various prompting strategies, our key findings includes: (1) incorporating abstracted context significantly enhances vulnerability detection effectiveness; (2) different models benefit from distinct levels of abstraction depending on their code understanding capabilities; and (3) capturing program behavior through program analysis for general LLM-based code analysis tasks can be a direction that requires further attention.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Context-Enhanced Vulnerability Detection Based on Large Language Model" introduces a novel approach, PacVD, to improve the accuracy of vulnerability detection using Large Language Models (LLMs). PacVD combines program analysis with LLMs by extracting contextual information at various levels of abstraction using primitive API information. The authors use control flow analysis and data flow analysis to generate abstraction. Specifically, the authors extract information regarding control branches containing API calls, count API call occurrences, and identify variables affected by API operations. They test different levels of abstraction with GPT-4, DeepSeek, and CodeLLaMA with various prompting strategies. The results show that incorporating abstracted context significantly enhances vulnerability detection, the optimal level of abstraction depends on the specific model and the type of vulnerability, and that API abstraction is particularly effective when combined with appropriate prompting strategies. They found that their approach improves F1 score by up to 9.25% using Chain-of-Thought prompting strategy with DeepSeek-R1.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of leveraging program analysis to extract contextual information, specifically via primitive API abstraction, to enhance LLM-based vulnerability detection has moderate novelty. Previous work has explored using LLMs and deep learning models for vulnerability detection, and some have used program analysis to extract code features. The specific combination of primitive API abstraction and its different levels, along with the empirical evaluation of its impact on various LLMs with diverse prompting strategies, gives this work a unique aspect. This is incremental, however, and not a fundamentally disruptive innovation.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and significant problem: improving the accuracy and reducing false positives in LLM-based vulnerability detection. The results demonstrate that the proposed context-enhanced approach, PacVD, outperforms baseline methods across different LLMs and prompting strategies, indicating a practical benefit. While the improvements are not earth-shattering, they contribute to the ongoing effort to make LLM-based security tools more reliable. The detailed evaluation of different abstraction levels and their interaction with LLMs provides useful insights for practitioners on how to best configure these systems. The study offers guidance in a rapidly evolving space.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined approach:** PacVD is clearly described and easy to understand.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The experimental evaluation is quite thorough, using multiple LLMs, abstraction levels, and prompting strategies. The use of a real-world C/C++ vulnerability dataset adds credibility.
    *   **Insightful results:** The paper provides several valuable insights, including the importance of API contextual information, the optimal balance between abstraction and noise, and the impact of abstraction levels on different vulnerability types and LLMs.
    *   **Practical implications:** The paper offers practical guidance for organizations implementing LLM-based vulnerability detection.
    *   **Open-source data and details:** The availability of the datasets and experimental details is a positive aspect, promoting reproducibility and further research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While PacVD improves upon baselines, the F1-score gains are not extremely dramatic in all cases.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation focuses primarily on C/C++ code and a specific set of vulnerabilities. The generalizability of the results to other programming languages and vulnerability types is not fully explored.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the API Abstraction is designed to be computationally efficient compared to whole-repository analysis, the paper lacks a thorough discussion and comparison of the computational cost of PacVD versus other approaches, including the time required for program analysis and LLM inference.
    *   **API Coverage:** While API are selected based on fundamental security operations and prior research, the list of covered API is not comprehensive, and may miss vulnerabilities stemming from other types of API calls.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** The paper offers some explanations for the observed results but lacks a formal theoretical justification for the effectiveness of the API abstraction method.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings will likely influence future research in the field of LLM-based vulnerability detection, particularly in the use of program analysis techniques to extract contextual information. The study also provides a valuable benchmark for evaluating new approaches.

**Overall:** This is a solid piece of research that makes a worthwhile contribution to the field of LLM-based vulnerability detection. The paper is well-written, the approach is sound, and the evaluation is thorough. While the improvements in detection accuracy are not revolutionary, they are meaningful and the insights gained from the study are valuable. It provides an excellent starting point for further exploration of context-aware LLM-based security tools.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The score of 7 reflects the paper's positive contributions but also acknowledges its limitations. The novelty is present but incremental. The results are significant, but the gains, while valuable, are not transformative. The comprehensive evaluation, insightful results, and practical implications are strong positives, while the limited scope, limited evaluation of computational cost and lack of a theoretical justification hold it back from a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Do Large Language Models know who did what to whom?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16884v1)
- **Authors**: Joseph M. Denning, Xiaohan, Guo, Bryor Snefjella, Idan A. Blank
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are commonly criticized for not understanding language. However, many critiques focus on cognitive abilities that, in humans, are distinct from language processing. Here, we instead study a kind of understanding tightly linked to language: inferring who did what to whom (thematic roles) in a sentence. Does the central training objective of LLMs-word prediction-result in sentence representations that capture thematic roles? In two experiments, we characterized sentence representations in four LLMs. In contrast to human similarity judgments, in LLMs the overall representational similarity of sentence pairs reflected syntactic similarity but not whether their agent and patient assignments were identical vs. reversed. Furthermore, we found little evidence that thematic role information was available in any subset of hidden units. However, some attention heads robustly captured thematic roles, independently of syntax. Therefore, LLMs can extract thematic roles but, relative to humans, this information influences their representations more weakly.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Do Large Language Models know who did what to whom?"

**Summary:**

This paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) can extract and represent thematic roles (i.e., who did what to whom) from sentences based solely on their word prediction training objective.  The authors perform two experiments. In Experiment 1, they compared the similarity of sentences with shared or reversed thematic roles using sentence representations from four LLMs (BERT, GPT2-Small, Llama2, and Persimmon) and human judgments.  They found that LLMs prioritized syntactic similarity over thematic roles, whereas human judgments were driven by thematic role understanding.  Experiment 2 explored whether thematic roles were represented in subsets of hidden units or in attention heads. They found limited evidence of thematic role representation in subsets of hidden units but stronger evidence of thematic role information in attention heads, sometimes exceeding human performance. The authors conclude that while LLMs can extract thematic roles, this information has a weaker influence on their overall sentence representations compared to humans.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The study's novelty lies primarily in its focus on thematic roles as a specific, measurable aspect of language understanding in LLMs and in its comparison to human performance. Previous work has probed LLMs for various linguistic capabilities, but this paper uniquely tackles how thematic roles are processed. The finding that LLMs prioritize syntax over semantics (thematic roles) in their overall representations is a key novel finding. Moreover, the study provides a nuanced view, demonstrating that attention heads can capture thematic role information better than hidden units.
*   **Significance:**  The paper's significance stems from its attempt to bridge the gap between LLM capabilities and human language processing. By focusing on thematic roles, the authors pinpoint a specific area where LLMs diverge from human behavior, even though the capacity to extract this information exists. This has implications for how we interpret LLM representations and compare them to human cognitive processes. This challenges the prevalent notion that high performance on tasks like word prediction necessarily equates to human-like language understanding. By emphasizing that thematic roles are key linguistic element, independent of more higher-level processes like common sense reasoning, the paper offers a valuable correction to some prior critiques.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined research question:** The paper has a clear, focused question that it systematically investigates.
    *   **Controlled experiments:** The experimental design is rigorous, particularly Experiment 2, with carefully constructed stimuli that minimize reliance on heuristics.
    *   **Multiple models:** Testing across several LLMs (BERT, GPT2, Llama2, Persimmon) strengthens the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Comparison to human data:**  The inclusion of human judgment data provides a crucial benchmark for evaluating LLM performance.
    *   **Nuanced analysis:** Investigating both hidden units and attention heads adds depth to the analysis and reveals more about the models' internal workings.
    *   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly articulates its methodology and findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited probing methods:** While the authors used a classification-based probing approach, other probing techniques might reveal different aspects of thematic role representation.
    *   **Lack of mechanistic explanation:**  The paper identifies *that* attention heads capture thematic roles, but it does not fully explain *how* this is achieved within the attention mechanism. A detailed analysis of the specific attention patterns involved could enhance the paper.
    *   **Simplification:** The focus on just "agent" and "patient" is a simplified depiction of thematic roles. More fine-grained roles might reveal richer representations or differences.
    *   **Limited human behavioral data:** The human studies were fairly simple similarity ratings and may not reflect the full complexity of human sentence processing.

**Overall:** The paper is a solid contribution to the field of LLM research, offering a valuable insight into how these models represent thematic roles. It's well-executed, rigorously analyzed, and makes a meaningful point about the differences between LLM and human language understanding. While not groundbreaking in the sense of completely rewriting our understanding of LLMs, it provides a useful, incremental advancement and refines our understanding of their internal workings.

**Score: 7.5**

Rationale: The paper offers a novel contribution that clarifies how LLMs represent thematic roles within sentence representations. The careful controls, comparison against human data, and analysis of attention heads contribute to the study's rigor. The significance is evident by providing insights into the differences between how LLMs and humans process and represent language, even in a simple semantic task. Limitations involve that further investigation of different probing methods may be used.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Tracing Thought: Using Chain-of-Thought Reasoning to Identify the LLM Behind AI-Generated Text
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16913v1)
- **Authors**: Shifali Agrahari, Sanasam Ranbir Singh
- **Abstract**: In recent years, the detection of AI-generated text has become a critical area of research due to concerns about academic integrity, misinformation, and ethical AI deployment. This paper presents COT Fine-tuned, a novel framework for detecting AI-generated text and identifying the specific language model. responsible for generating the text. We propose a dual-task approach, where Task A involves classifying text as AI-generated or human-written, and Task B identifies the specific LLM behind the text. The key innovation of our method lies in the use of Chain-of-Thought reasoning, which enables the model to generate explanations for its predictions, enhancing transparency and interpretability. Our experiments demonstrate that COT Fine-tuned achieves high accuracy in both tasks, with strong performance in LLM identification and human-AI classification. We also show that the CoT reasoning process contributes significantly to the models effectiveness and interpretability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces COT_Finetuned, a novel framework for detecting AI-generated text and identifying the specific Large Language Model (LLM) responsible for its creation. The approach utilizes Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning to generate explanations for its predictions, aiming to improve transparency and interpretability. It frames the problem as a dual-task: (A) classifying text as AI-generated or human-written, and (B) identifying the specific LLM behind AI-generated text.  The method involves fine-tuning a pre-trained language model using a combined loss function that accounts for both tasks.  Experiments compare the performance of BERT and RoBERTa, with and without CoT, demonstrating improved F1 scores when CoT is incorporated.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The use of Chain-of-Thought reasoning for AI-generated text detection isn't entirely novel, as CoT has been explored in other NLP tasks for enhancing reasoning and explainability. The "novelty" lies in the *specific* application of CoT to this particular dual-task setting of AI-generated text detection *and* LLM identification. However, similar applications may already exist in the broad landscape of detecting machine-generated content.  The dual-task framing is a decent contribution.

**Significance:** The detection of AI-generated content is undeniably a significant area due to concerns about misinformation, academic integrity, and responsible AI deployment. A system capable of not just detecting AI-generated text but also identifying the source model could be valuable. If the experiments stand up to scrutiny on diverse datasets, the result can be useful.

**Strengths:**

*   **Dual-Task Approach:** Addressing both detection and source identification in a single framework is more practical than dealing with them as separate problems.
*   **Chain-of-Thought Reasoning:** The integration of CoT attempts to improve the interpretability of the model's decisions, a crucial aspect for building trust in AI detection systems. If the explanations generated are meaningful and helpful for understanding the classification rationale, it's a notable plus.
*   **Improved Performance:** The experimental results suggest that incorporating CoT improves the F1 scores for both AI-vs-Human classification and LLM identification tasks.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:** The basic approach of fine-tuning language models for text classification is standard. CoT adds a layer, but its novelty is moderate.
*   **Experimental Rigor:** The experimental setup needs more detail, particularly regarding the size and nature of the training data, CoT prompt engineering (the quality of prompts is crucial for CoT's success), and the number of LLMs being distinguished. The results presented are brief.
*   **Dataset Bias:** The paper should acknowledge potential biases present in datasets used for training and evaluation. Performance might degrade on unseen datasets or different domains. If the models have been trained on the types of review text used in Table 7 before, the experimental results are likely to be biased and inflated.
*   **Interpretability Evaluation:** The paper claims CoT enhances interpretability but doesn't include a concrete evaluation of the quality or usefulness of the generated explanations. Are the explanations accurate? Do they help users understand *why* the model made a particular decision?
*   **Limited Comparison:** The paper only compared results of Roberta and Bert models. There are many available models that should have been tried and discussed.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the above analysis, the paper presents a potentially valuable, but not groundbreaking, contribution. The application of CoT to this specific dual-task is a positive, and the initial results are promising. However, the limited novelty, questions about experimental rigor, and lack of interpretability evaluation hold it back.  The paper could be significantly improved by expanding the experimental evaluation, adding a formal assessment of explanation quality, and more thoroughly discussing the limitations. Given these factors, a score of **6** is appropriate.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### IberBench: LLM Evaluation on Iberian Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16921v1)
- **Authors**: José Ángel González, Ian Borrego Obrador, Álvaro Romo Herrero, Areg Mikael Sarvazyan, Mara Chinea-Ríos, Angelo Basile, Marc Franco-Salvador
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) remain difficult to evaluate comprehensively, particularly for languages other than English, where high-quality data is often limited. Existing benchmarks and leaderboards are predominantly English-centric, with only a few addressing other languages. These benchmarks fall short in several key areas: they overlook the diversity of language varieties, prioritize fundamental Natural Language Processing (NLP) capabilities over tasks of industrial relevance, and are static. With these aspects in mind, we present IberBench, a comprehensive and extensible benchmark designed to assess LLM performance on both fundamental and industry-relevant NLP tasks, in languages spoken across the Iberian Peninsula and Ibero-America. IberBench integrates 101 datasets from evaluation campaigns and recent benchmarks, covering 22 task categories such as sentiment and emotion analysis, toxicity detection, and summarization. The benchmark addresses key limitations in current evaluation practices, such as the lack of linguistic diversity and static evaluation setups by enabling continual updates and community-driven model and dataset submissions moderated by a committee of experts. We evaluate 23 LLMs ranging from 100 million to 14 billion parameters and provide empirical insights into their strengths and limitations. Our findings indicate that (i) LLMs perform worse on industry-relevant tasks than in fundamental ones, (ii) performance is on average lower for Galician and Basque, (iii) some tasks show results close to random, and (iv) in other tasks LLMs perform above random but below shared task systems. IberBench offers open-source implementations for the entire evaluation pipeline, including dataset normalization and hosting, incremental evaluation of LLMs, and a publicly accessible leaderboard.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces IberBench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on Iberian languages (Spanish, Portuguese, Catalan, Basque, and Galician) and various regional dialects.  IberBench addresses the limitations of existing benchmarks, which are predominantly English-centric and often fail to adequately capture the linguistic diversity and industry-relevant tasks specific to Iberian languages. The benchmark comprises 101 datasets covering 22 task categories, including sentiment analysis, toxicity detection, and summarization. The authors evaluated 23 LLMs, ranging in size from 100 million to 14 billion parameters, and presented empirical insights into their strengths and weaknesses.  The results indicate that LLMs generally underperform on industry-relevant tasks compared to fundamental ones, and perform relatively worse in Galician and Basque. IberBench also offers an open-source evaluation pipeline and a public leaderboard for continuous evaluation and community contributions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its targeted focus on Iberian languages, a significantly under-resourced area in LLM evaluation. Existing benchmarks often overlook the linguistic nuances and specific challenges presented by these languages and their dialects. The inclusion of industry-relevant tasks is also a valuable contribution, shifting the focus beyond purely academic exercises to more practical applications.  The incorporation of a community-driven, continually updated benchmark is another step towards more sustainable and representative LLM evaluations. However, the reliance on existing datasets, while practical, limits the originality of the dataset itself. The technical aspects of the evaluation pipeline using `lm-evaluation-harness` are also not entirely novel, though its adaptation to Iberian languages and sequence labeling tasks is a clear contribution.
* **Significance:** IberBench has the potential to significantly impact the field by driving research and development specifically tailored for Iberian language technologies. The open-source nature of the benchmark and the inclusion of a community-driven aspect encourage collaboration and contribute to the advancement of NLP in these languages. Providing a clear benchmark and leaderboard will also help to direct development of new models and fine-tuning strategies. The analysis of LLM performance across different tasks, languages, and model types provides valuable insights that can inform future research directions. The results of the empirical study highlight important gaps in LLM capabilities, such as the struggle with industry-relevant tasks and the lower performance in certain languages like Galician and Basque. This information can be used to prioritize future development efforts.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Focus on Under-Resourced Languages:** Addresses a crucial gap in the LLM evaluation landscape.
    * **Inclusion of Industry-Relevant Tasks:** Moves beyond purely academic exercises.
    * **Community-Driven and Continually Updated:** Promotes collaboration and long-term relevance.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation Pipeline:** Open-source implementation facilitates reproducibility and further development.
    * **Detailed Analysis of Results:** Provides valuable insights into LLM strengths and weaknesses.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Existing Datasets:** While practical, limits the originality and potential for novel evaluation scenarios. A greater focus on creating benchmark-specific tasks could boost the novelty of the work.
    * **Limited Originality of Evaluation Framework:** The adaptation of `lm-evaluation-harness` is well-justified, but not a major innovation in itself.
    * **Incomplete Coverage of Dialects/Varieties:**  While aiming for linguistic diversity, the benchmark acknowledges limitations in covering all regional variations of Spanish and Portuguese, particularly those from Ibero-America.
    * **Lack of comparison against state-of-the-art shared tasks:** The paper compared the performance of LLMs against the best models in a specific task, but a direct comparison of LLMs against shared task models using the same configuration (zero-shot or few-shot) would have provided a more meaningful insight in understanding how LLMs are performing compared to models trained in a supervised fashion for that specific task.
    * **English-centric analysis:** Even if English is added as an Iberian language, its inclusion complicates and skews the analysis because the datasets are not comparable (it is only included in a very specific task).

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of NLP by providing a valuable resource for evaluating LLMs in under-resourced Iberian languages. Its focus on industry-relevant tasks and community-driven approach positions it as a practical tool for advancing language technologies in these regions.  While the reliance on existing datasets and the use of a pre-existing evaluation framework limit the novelty, the adaptation and application of these resources to a specific linguistic and application context justify a positive assessment. The findings are important and provide directions for future research and development.

**Score: 7**

Rationale: While highly valuable and addressing a clear need, the paper's novelty is primarily in its specific focus and integration of existing resources rather than fundamental methodological innovation or the creation of entirely new, transformative datasets/techniques. The insights provided from the empirical study are helpful, but further analysis such as comparison to shared-task models would increase the impact of the work.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10
